Kept at 480, it leaves the text about 320 px at 100%, about 40 characters of Georgia at 17 px. When even both strips leave less than 480 (a 900 by 600 window from 175%, 1100 by 700 at 200%), the pane's own side padding narrows from 40 to 16 px, which gives the text 48 px more there.

**The chapter list folds first.** It is used to change chapters, which happens between reviews, not during one; the side panel holds the review selector, the focused suggestion, the comments and History, used all through a review. Both shrink a little before either folds (to 150 and 200 px), as a fold costs more than a few pixels of width. The default 1440 by 940 window never folds the side panel.

**Drawers, not columns that push the prose.** A folded column opened in place would take its width back from the prose: at 200% in a 900 by 600 window, the side panel at 240 px would leave the centre column 170 px and the text about 60. A drawer over the prose's edge leaves the prose as it was, and closes as soon as the author presses in the prose. It is under the first-use hint and the notification cards, so neither is ever hidden behind it; the hint keeps to the side panel's column wherever it is drawn, which with the panel folded is the drawer's place beside the strip, over the top right of the prose. A drawer has the page's background, which is what lies under the column in place, so the panel, faded while a review runs, does not let the prose show through it (`ui.3` review).

**The hint gives way to the prose with the side panel folded** (`ui.3` review). As first built, the hint lay over the prose until Got it: at 200% in a 900 by 600 window over the right 60% of it, its whole height, and over the paragraph editor's text, Cancel and Done wherever the paragraph sat, so Done could not be clicked, and over most of either drawer when one was open. That undid `apply.12`'s rule that the hint never meets the chapter's text, and the authors who zoom to 200% are the ones who most need the text clear. Two other ways were weighed. A full-width row under the toolbar, pushing the prose down, covers nothing, but the hint across the window's width is 91 to 111 CSS px tall, and with five chip categories the prose there has 287, 166, 112 and 68 px at 125%, 150%, 175% and 200%: from 175% the row would leave no prose. Showing it only in the Suggestions drawer would leave an author who never opens the drawer without it. So the hint still shows, once, where `ui.3` put it, and gives way as soon as the author goes on: it waits while a paragraph is open for editing, and the next press anywhere but on the hint, review key or drawer opened sets it aside while the panel stays folded, until the next start, writing nothing (section 7, First-use hint). The press or key that makes the first change comes before the hint shows, so the author always sees it once a session; with the panel in place it covers only the top of the panel, as before, and stays until Got it or a save.

**Escape never closes a drawer along with something else** (`ui.3` review). The find bar's box closes the bar on Escape in its own handler, which runs at React's root before keyboard.ts's window listener, and the Bulk menu and the legend close on it in window listeners added after keyboard.ts's. keyboard.ts found the bar already closed and closed the drawer as well, or closed the drawer under an open menu. An Escape something else has taken (`defaultPrevented`) now leaves the drawer alone, and so does one pressed while the menu or the legend is open (`popupTakesEscape`); the two also take the press they close on, so the order of the listeners does not matter. Left as it was before `ui.3`: with no drawer open, one Escape still closes the find bar and the Bulk menu or the legend together when both are open, since keyboard.ts closes the bar before their listeners run.

**Script, not media queries.** The widths depend on the window's width only, which CSS media queries could have read, but the strips are buttons with state (which drawer is open, the focus handed back on Escape), and the hint has to be placed again when the columns move. So the page measures `.body` with a ResizeObserver and computes the layout with the pure `columnLayout`, tested in node with fast-check properties (the widths add up, the centre keeps 480 px unless both columns are folded, a column folds only when nothing less would do and never as the window widens). It renders inside the observer's callback with `flushSync`, before the frame is painted, so a zoom step never shows the old layout. The widths reach app.css as custom properties on `.body`, whose defaults there are the `clamp()` widths of round 5 for the first moment.

**A verification note.** In a `dev:clean` window that Windows reports hidden (`document.visibilityState` is "hidden" while the window is covered, C.30), no frame is drawn and no ResizeObserver reports until something asks for a frame, so a zoom step's new layout appears only then; the drive scripts take a screenshot first. In such a window Chromium also does not open a select's list on a click, so the selector was checked by a real click, which takes the focus, and type-ahead.

### C.59 The split is a share of the height under the box, and the usual split is none (`ui.6`, 2026-10-18)

The task asked for the split "as a fraction of the panel height". It is the list's share of the height the list, the handle and the region below share: the panel less the review selector (44 CSS px), the focused suggestion's box (139) and the footer (40), all three of fixed height. A share of the whole panel would keep the line at the same share of a height that also holds those 223 px, so as the window, the zoom or the chips changed the panel's height, the two regions' own proportion would change; a share of their own height keeps it.

**The usual split is null, not 0.55.** The usual split is no fixed line: the list takes what it needs up to 55%, so a chapter with a few suggestions leaves the rest to the comments. A stored 0.55 would give such a list a region mostly empty. Double-click and Enter put the usual split back by writing null. A split the author sets is a fixed line, the list's region keeping its share however many rows it holds, so the handle stays where it was put when the chips or another review change the count.

**The limits are measured, and CSS draws the line.** Three rows each side depend on a row's height (22 CSS px here), the list's heading and padding (37), the region below's padding (20) and the handle's 7, which come from the fonts and the rules. The page measures them after every render and whenever the regions change size, and hands them to app.css as `--split-min` and `--split-gap` beside the share (`--split`); the list's height is `clamp(var(--split-min), calc(var(--split) * 100%), calc(100% - var(--split-gap)))`, which `splitHeight` in `src/shared/ui/split.ts` repeats for the keys and the drag. So a resize redraws the line with no script, even in a window Windows reports hidden, where no ResizeObserver reports until a frame is drawn (C.58's verification note); only the handle's value for a screen reader waits for one. app.css holds the measures at 100% as defaults until the page has its own.

**When the limits cross** (`ui.6` review, 2026-10-18). Three rows each side need the list's 103 CSS px, the handle's 7 and the region below's 86: 196 px under the box at 100%. The handle moves by the height under the box less 196 px, and not at all below that. As first landed, the least won wherever the limits crossed, so any split set drew the list at its three rows, and section 7 said each region kept three rows "whatever the split". The review found that a split set then left the comments less room than the usual split, which gives a full list 55%, in the windows where `ui.5` said the split was needed. Measured on the commit under review in a `dev:clean` window on Chapter 01 with 34 suggestions over 13 categories and 15 comments, the viewport set to the inside of each window: in the smallest window, 900 by 600, at 100% (884x561, the chips and the status bar on two rows each, 180 px under the box), a split set in the default window to favour the comments (Home, 0.18) drew the list at 103 and left the region below 50 px of what it scrolls, where the usual split drew 99 and left 54; End moved the list 4 px, and Home not at all. In the drawer of a 1000 by 900 window at 150% (656x574, the chips on three rows, 131 px), the same split left the region below 1 px, the usual split 32; at 884x545 (164 px), 34 against 47.

**The least is never more than 55%.** The clamp's least is `min(var(--split-min), 55%)`, and `splitMin` in split.ts is the same. Where the panel is too short for three rows each side, a split set draws the line where the usual split draws it under a full list, whatever the share, so it never leaves the region below less; between 187 and 196 px at 100%, where the list's three rows are under 55%, the list keeps them and the region below has a few pixels more than under the usual split. A list too short to fill its 55% still has more room under a split set than under the usual split, as in any window, since a split set holds its share however many rows the list has. The other choice was to ignore the split there, as the one scroller does; that needs the page to switch on a measure, which a window Windows reports hidden takes only at its next frame (C.58's verification note), where the cap in `clamp()` needs no script.

**A handle that cannot move an arrow step is no control.** Below 220 px at 100% (196 and one 24 px step) the handle has `aria-disabled`, leaves the Tab order and is drawn as the plain line, with no grip and the usual pointer, and a press, a drag, a double-click and its keys do nothing (`splitMovable`; section 7, The split), so a keyboard user does not reach a control that does nothing. It had been focusable with 4 px of travel. In the smallest window at 100% the handle moves only when the chips and the status bar take one row each (228 px, 32 px of travel); with the chips on one row and the status bar on two (209 px) it would move 13 px, with the chips on two rows and the status bar on one (199 px) 3, and below that not at all. In the default window at 150%, with this chapter's chips on two rows, the 243 px under the box let it move; Home took the list to its three rows, 100.67 px there.

Checked on the fix in the same `dev:clean` window. At 884x561, the split 0.18 and the usual split both drew the list at 99.05 px and left the region below 54; the handle had `aria-disabled`, tabindex -1, no grip and the default pointer, and 55% as its value and both limits; a real drag of 60 px on it and a double-click changed nothing, wrote nothing and left the focus on the page; with the focus on it, Home, End and Enter did nothing and ArrowDown moved the focus to the next suggestion. In real windows sized with `SetWindowPos`, 900 by 600 at 100% (884x561 inside) gave the same, and 1000 by 900 at 150% (984x861 inside, zoomed through `view:zoom`, the side panel in its drawer, 136 px) drew the list at 75 and left the region below 34 with the split set and without. At 884x611 (230 px) the handle moved: Home to 103, ArrowDown 24 px, End to 137.09 (230.11 less 93), a real drag of 60 px up stopped at 103 and one of 30 px down reached 133, and a double-click put back the usual split; shrunk to 884x561 with the split at End it was still and drew 99.05, and grown back it drew 137.09 again, and Tab from the last row reached it. The default window at 100% was as before: 103 to 475, and drags of 60 px up and 30 down moved the line by as much. Screenshots of the still handle in light and dark, looked at by eye, are in the session's scratch folder `u6f/out-after3`.

**540, not 420.** The task named the 420 px fallback of the first `panel.1`; since C.49 the one scroller is a window less than 540 CSS px high inside, and the handle follows that media query.

**Six places.** The share is written to six decimal places, within a hundredth of a pixel in any window; with four, a 24 px arrow step came out at 23.97 px in the default window.

### C.60 A comment that loses its paragraph keeps its number (`ui.7`, 2026-10-18)

`rebaseReview` gave a comment whose paragraph and quote had both left the file `paragraph: null`, the value a comment about the whole chapter has, so the side panel listed it among the open comments as "chapter" and nothing said where it had been. `ui.7` asks for "unanchored (was paragraph 12)" and a place in the list by that number, so the comment now keeps the index it had in `wasParagraph` (section 3). `paragraph` stays null, so the margin, the `n` key, Edit text, `remapAfterCommit` and `repairCommentAnchors` treat it as before, as having no paragraph; only the side panel reads the new field (`isUnanchored`, `commentPlace`, `sortComments`), and Edit text for its words (`commentEditBlocked`).

**The number is the one it had.** It counts in the text the comment lost its place in, as Unanchored's suggestions keep theirs ("Paragraph 4 in the original review"), and neither a later change on disk nor a save renumbers it. A deletion above the lost paragraph in the same change therefore leaves the number too high against the page's numbers: in the `ui.7` window, with "You knew." (5) and "Tomorrow we move the ewes" (8) deleted together, the second comment read "was paragraph 8" and sat where paragraph 7 now is, and so was listed after the resolved comment on the new paragraph 7. Working out where the lost paragraph would be in the new text, from the paragraphs either side that are still found, was left out: it would make "was" mean "would now be", the task's words are "the paragraph it last had", and the comment is still near the place it names.

**Nothing anchors it again.** As before, a comment without a paragraph is passed over by the next rebase, so it stays unanchored if the paragraph comes back.

**Older reviews.** A comment that lost its paragraph before this has no `wasParagraph` and still reads "chapter"; nothing in the review tells it from a comment about the whole chapter.

**Rendered in a test.** The side panel is checked by rendering `SidePanel` with `react-dom/server` in vitest's Node environment, with no DOM: the store's initial state is enough, and the effects, which measure and scroll, do not run on the server. The test file is named `*.render.test.ts`: tsconfig.web.json, which has JSX and the DOM library, type-checks such files, and tsconfig.node.json, which has neither, leaves them out.

### C.61 Quiet test windows (`dev.1`, 2026-10-18)

The verification runs kept opening the app in front of whatever the author was working in: the window took the foreground when it first showed, and a second launch or a held close brought it forward again. A development switch, `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_DEV_QUIET_WINDOW` (`npm run dev:clean -- --quiet`), read in `src/main/devSwitches.ts` and ignored by a packaged build like every other switch (C.39), keeps a test window out of the way. Everything it changes is in `src/main/foreground.ts`, which is also the only file in `src/main` that shows, restores or focuses the window, as a test checks. No CHANGELOG line: nothing changes for anyone who runs the app.

**Shown without the foreground.** `showInactive()` takes the place of `show()` at the first show (C.36), and nothing calls `focus()`, `moveTop()` or `restore()`; on Windows `showInactive()` is `SW_SHOWNOACTIVATE`. A window first shown this way keeps its place in the z-order, so a launcher that pushed it to the bottom while it was still hidden never sees it in front. The second launch (C.40) and the close guard's reveal (7.7) go through `bringForward`, which for a quiet window is `showInactive()` when it is not minimised and nothing when it is. The close guard does not flash a quiet window's taskbar button either: the flash asks for the attention the focus would have taken, so a check of the flash runs without the switch.

**Left minimised** (review fix, 2026-10-18). At first `bringForward` restored a minimised quiet window with `showInactive()`, since `SW_SHOWNOACTIVATE` also restores a window without activating it, and the one run of `dev.1`'s window check saw the foreground stay put and the window come back above the others. That was one run of an effect that comes and goes. The reviewer, with another program's window in front and second launches from a background process, saw the restored window take the foreground once in three tries and hold it, at z-order index 19 above the window that had had it, and come back at the bottom with the foreground untouched the other two times; a held close restored it at the bottom. In the fix's own check of the code before it, with a cover window of ours in front, six second launches, three from a background process and three from the cover's process, never moved the foreground, and the window came back above the cover twice and at the bottom four times. The likely cause is that Chromium's single-instance code, when a second copy hands over, gives the running copy the right to take the foreground (`AllowSetForegroundWindow`, which `electron.exe` imports), after which Windows may activate or raise a window it restores. So a minimised quiet window is now left minimised by both, and a test driver that needs it restores it itself, from a process that has no such right: the helper's `restore` (`ShowWindow(SW_SHOWNOACTIVATE)`, then `HWND_BOTTOM`). On the fix, in a `dev:clean --quiet` window with another program's window in front: three second launches left the minimised window minimised and two left the shown one where it was, at the same place in the z-order, with the foreground unmoved; a WM_CLOSE with typed text at risk left the minimised window minimised while the page put its question up, and no native box came; the helper's restore, five times, brought it back at the bottom without the foreground, and a CDP screenshot then showed the page.

**Outside the switch.** The switch covers what the app does to its own window, not the windows Windows draws for it. The native box of a held close (7.7, `dialog.showMessageBox` over the window) comes up in front. In the fix's check, on the code before it (the fix changes nothing here for a window that is not minimised), with the page paused in the debugger and WM_CLOSE posted to the quiet window at the bottom (index 266) under the cover, the box came 3 s later, took the foreground and pulled the window up to index 20, just under itself; when the page answered and the box was taken away, the window kept the foreground at index 19 until the cover took it back. It did so both times tried; the reviewer saw the window raised both times and the foreground taken once in two. The folder picker (`showOpenDialog`, Change folder) and Explorer (`shell.openPath`, Show backups) are separate windows that the switch cannot keep quiet either; they were not tried. A check that provokes any of them brings the window forward on purpose and sends it back after (`shots-app.cjs front`, then `back <hwnd>`). Skipping the box under the switch was considered and not done: the box is what a check of an unresponsive page looks at, and a quiet window without it would be checking something the author never sees.

**Rendering while covered.** Chromium works out from the desktop's windows whether its window is wholly covered (the `CalculateNativeWinOcclusion` feature) and then treats the page as hidden: `document.visibilityState` is `hidden`, no animation frames come and timers are throttled, the likely cause of C.30. Under the switch that feature is disabled on the command line at module scope, since Chromium reads its features when the app gets ready, and the window's `backgroundThrottling` is off. Without the switch, on this machine, a covered page was hidden and gave no animation frame within 2 s, but `Page.captureScreenshot` itself still returned in about 140 ms after 3 s and after 20 s covered, and showed a change made while covered, so the hanging screenshots the `ui.5` run saw did not happen again; a driver that waits for animation frames before a shot, as `shots.mjs` does, is what a hidden page holds up. With the switch the covered page stayed visible, its frames came, and a screenshot returned in 134 ms.

**The launchers.** The agents' scratch launchers (`shots-app.cjs`, `window-run.cjs`) pass `--quiet` and, through their user32 helper, push the window to the bottom of the z-order with `SetWindowPos(hwnd, HWND_BOTTOM, 0, 0, 0, 0, SWP_NOMOVE | SWP_NOSIZE | SWP_NOACTIVATE)` as soon as it exists, while it is still hidden, and again once it shows. An agent brings the window forward only for a step that needs real OS input and sends it back straight after; Windows refuses the foreground to a process in the background, so the helper joins the input queue of the window in front for that one call. A driver that takes the foreground, even for a stand-in window of its own, stops doing so once the author is at the machine (`GetLastInputInfo` says when the last input came): in the review fix's check the author came back mid-run, and the stand-in, taking the foreground back after each step, took it from them three times in 8 s before it was closed. A check that needs the page to believe it has the focus turns on CDP's `Emulation.setFocusEmulationEnabled`: without it, in a quiet window, `document.hasFocus()` is false and a focused button does not match `:focus`.

### C.62 DevTools input gives a covered window's page the focus (verification note, `setup.7`, 2026-10-18)

A quiet test window (C.61) never has the foreground, so its page should believe it has no focus: `dev.1` found `document.hasFocus()` false there until focus emulation was turned on. That holds only until the first mouse event sent over DevTools. In `setup.7`'s window check, after trusted clicks through `Input.dispatchMouseEvent` (opening a chapter, then Setup), `document.hasFocus()` was true with the window at the bottom of the z-order and another program's window in front, and with no focus emulation in the session. Two things follow for a check.

- **A focus event needs the page to lose the focus first.** Bringing that window forward fired no `focus` event on the page, since it already had the focus; sending it back fired `blur`. Only the next time it came forward did `focus` fire, and with it the Setup screen's re-check. A check of what the window coming back does sends it forward and back once before it starts counting, or installs its probe before any DevTools input.
- **The clipboard is the system's.** With the page focused, a Copy clicked over DevTools in a window at the bottom may well write the system clipboard, the author's, without the window ever coming forward; it was not tried, for that reason. A check that clicks a Copy saves the clipboard's text first and puts it back after (only text: a picture or the HTML of a browser copy is not put back); a check of a Copy the clipboard refuses stubs `navigator.clipboard.writeText` in the page to reject, so the clipboard is never touched.

### C.63 Test the connection is a review's exchange with a one-line prompt, and answers rather than throws (`setup.8`, 2026-10-18)

- **One exchange for both.** `runCliReview` and the new `runCliTest` share `exchange` in `claudeCli.ts`: start Claude Code, send `initialize`, the one user message, read up to the `result`, always end the process. The test's `initialize` carries the system prompt "Reply with the single word OK." and no schema, its message is "This is a connection test.", and its command line is `claudeArgs({model: 'haiku', maxTurns: 1})`: the review's base flags, `--model haiku --max-turns 1`, no `--effort` (Claude Code's own for the model) and no partial messages. Its `result` is read by `resultToError`, as a review's is (C.47), so a test fails wherever a review would and in the same words. Any answer that is not a failure counts: the test is of the connection, not of the model's obedience, so the word is not checked. The model is the first key of `modelUsage`, else the one the `system` init message named.
- **The time limit is the caller's.** `testConnection` (`providers/connectionTest.ts`, no electron import) runs either provider's `test`, aborts it at 60 s and answers the timeout at once rather than once the process has gone; the abort ends Claude Code's process tree, or OpenAI's request, as Cancel does for a review. Its controller is registered, so quitting ends a test too. Whatever happens comes back as `{ok: false, error}`, never as a thrown error; an error that is not the provider's own is "The test failed: …" with the provider's failure code, never `toAppError`'s file wording.
- **The duration is the app's clock**, from the request to the answer with Claude Code's start-up included, not the result's `duration_ms`, which counts only the request: it is what the author waits for.
- **`test-ok` is `success`.** The task's word predates `setup.6`, whose `nextLastFailure` takes one `success` event for a review and a connection test alike; a test that went through sends that (`testSetupEvent`), one that failed sends `failure` with its code, and a cancelled one nothing.
- **The real test** (2026-10-18, the one request the task allowed, from a `dev:clean --quiet` window on a scratch copy of the samples): this machine's Claude Code 2.1.302 on a Max plan answered in 1.7 s by the app's clock, 1758 ms from the trusted click to the line on screen, as `claude-haiku-5-5`, and sent a `rate_limit_event` with status `allowed_warning` and type `seven_day`, which the status bar then showed as "allowed_warning (seven_day)" in its warning colour, as it does after a review; the words for it are `setup.9`'s.
- **"Connect" in the render test's sign-in check.** `setupScreen.render.test.ts` treats a button labelled like "Connect Claude" as a sign-in; its pattern now leaves out the noun, so "Test the connection", which signs no one in, passes, and the test pins it as the only button that names a connection.
- **The note follows the credential** (`setup.8` review). The task's note, "which counts against your plan like any other", stood in every view the test is offered in, so with an API key the card said reviews are billed to the key "not to a Claude plan" and, a few lines under it, that the test counts against your plan (seen in a `dev:clean --claude-config-empty --api-key-dummy` window). `connectionTestNote` picks the note from the check's `credential`: the task's words on a Claude plan, "is billed to your API key" with an API key, "is billed through your cloud provider" for Bedrock, Vertex and the like, and "counts against your plan or API key" where the check failed, which leaves the credential unknown (5.6 item 5). Each ends "like any other", as the task's does.
- **Held, not disabled** (`setup.8` review). A focused button that becomes `disabled` loses the focus in Chromium, which leaves it on the page. Seen in the window: Test the connection focused and pressed with Space, the focus was on the page while the test ran and after; Check again the same once its check ended. `heldButton` (`src/renderer/state/heldButton.ts`) gives both `aria-disabled` and no click handler while their work runs, and `app.css` draws `button[aria-disabled='true']` as `button:disabled` and leaves it out of the hover and press colours. The handler matters for Check again: unlike the test, `useAi.check` does not refuse a second check while one runs. The tests run without a DOM, so the render test pins the markup (no `disabled`, `aria-disabled="true"`), a unit test the props and `setupEntry.test.ts` that both buttons take them; the focus itself was checked in the window.
- **Quitting, pinned for either provider** (`setup.8` review). The test that quitting ends a test passed without `testConnection` registering its controller, since the Claude exchange registers one of its own through `deps.track`. OpenAI's test only listens to the signal it is given, so quitting during it rests on that registration alone. `connectionTest.test.ts` now also runs a stub that only listens, and OpenAI's test against the mock's `slow` key, each with `track: registerController`: one controller while the test runs, `abortAll()` aborts it, and the answer is `cancelled` (at once for OpenAI, whose reply the mock holds for 7.2 s).

### C.64 Usage notices are Claude Code's own figures, worded from 80% (`setup.9`, 2026-10-18)

- **What Claude Code sends.** The `rate_limit_info` of a `rate_limit_event`, as the zod schema inside this machine's Claude Code 2.1.302 defines it and the Agent SDK 0.3.283's `SDKRateLimitInfo` documents it: `status` (`allowed`, `allowed_warning` or `rejected`), `resetsAt` (Unix seconds), `rateLimitType` (`five_hour`, `seven_day`, `seven_day_opus`, `seven_day_sonnet`, `seven_day_overage_included` or `overage`), `utilization`, `isUsingOverage`, and fields the app does not read (`overageStatus`, `overageResetsAt`, `overageDisabledReason`, `surpassedThreshold`, `unifiedWindows` and more). The schema's own description gives `utilization` as the fraction of the window used, "usually 0-1", and above 1 when usage runs past a limit. In 2.1.302 it comes only with `allowed_warning`, Claude Code's own warning (`setup.9` review, read from the program): the notice built from the response's headers (`Jl`) has no share, a `rejected` one is passed on as it is (`DS`), and the share is added only where Claude Code warns (`nh`), when Anthropic's servers say a threshold was passed, or, by its own table, from 90% of the 5-hour limit in the first 72% of the window and from 75%, 50% or 25% of the weekly limit in the first 60%, 35% or 15% of the week. `isUsingOverage` is set on a `rejected` notice only, when the plan's limit is refused and extra usage still allows the request. A share for every window on every notice exists only in `unifiedWindows`, which the schema marks `@internal`, so the app does not read it. `readRateLimit` keeps the status, the reset, `kind`, `utilization` and `usingOverage`, and leaves out a field of the wrong type rather than guessing; `test/helpers/fakeClaude.ts` sends that shape, with no share on its `allowed` and `rejected` notices (it sent 0.42 and 1 at first, which Claude Code never does). Nothing here was sent to Claude to learn it: the schema was read from the installed program, and no review or test ran.
- **`message` is gone.** It was `${status} (${rateLimitType})`, which the status bar showed as "allowed_warning (seven_day)" after `setup.8`'s real test (C.63). Nothing reads it now.
- **Why 80%.** Claude Code raises `allowed_warning` from thresholds that depend on how far into a window it is, so early in a week it warns well below 80%, and a status bar that warned on the status alone would warn for most of a week. `describeRateLimit` follows the share instead, taken to a tenth of a percent and then rounded down, as Claude Code words its own warning ("You've used 85% of your session limit"), so the two never differ, 79.6% says nothing and 99.5% reads 99%, never 100% of a limit not reached (`setup.9` review: rounded to the nearest, 0.796 read 80% and 0.995 read 100%); capped at 100%; and it says nothing without one, unless a limit is reached (`rejected`) or extra usage is paying, which it always says, since that may be billed. As the share comes only with Claude Code's warning (above), the notice from 80% shows only while Claude Code is warning: late in a window, even at 85 to 99% used, an `allowed` notice has no share and the bar says nothing until the limit is reached.
- **Names.** "5-hour limit", as the task gives it, where Claude Code says "session limit"; "weekly limit", "weekly Opus limit", "weekly Sonnet limit"; "weekly model limit" for `seven_day_overage_included`, a weekly limit for one model that Claude Code names after the model (2.1.302: "Fable limit") and counts, with the Opus and Sonnet limits, as a model's limit; "extra usage limit" for `overage`; "usage limit" for anything else. The weekly Sonnet limit's name depends on the plan, as in Claude Code (`setup.9` review): 2.1.302 names `seven_day_sonnet` "weekly limit" on a Pro or Enterprise plan (its `JZt`), where it is the account's weekly limit and its message is "You've hit your weekly limit", and "Sonnet limit" on any other or none known. The app does the same from the check's `subscriptionType` (Pro as `isProPlan` reads it, Enterprise likewise), in the status bar, its tooltip and both cards' advice, which on those plans is to wait: told "Haiku and Opus do not count against the weekly Sonnet limit" there, a Pro user, whom the app sets up with Sonnet (`setup.1`), would have been sent to models the same limit holds.
- **Time.** The computer's own clock, 24-hour, as "Decisions kept 14:02". A reset that is not today gives its day, since a weekly limit's "resets 09:00" would read as today: "tomorrow 09:00", the weekday within the week ("Sat 09:00"), else the date ("29 Oct 09:00"). Days are counted by calendar date, so a change of the clocks in between does not shift them. The tooltip says it in full with the time left, from now to the minute its clock time names, counted up to the whole minute, so it is always the difference between that clock time and the computer's (`untilPhrase`, `rateLimitTitle`); from a day on it gives days and whole hours, the minutes left to the clock time before it. It was counted down from the reset at first, which the docstring claimed never promised a reset sooner than it is, but did the opposite: the status bar reads the clock with its seconds, so for all but the first second of each minute the time left was a minute short of the clock time it followed ("It resets at 22:29, in 2 hours and 9 minutes" at 20:19:27), and past a day a whole hour short ("in 1 day and 1 hour" for 26 hours; `setup.9` review).
- **Expiry.** The notice is about one window; once its reset has passed it is out of date, so `describeRateLimit` gives null. The status bar's `UsageNotice` words it again every 30 s while it shows, which keeps the tooltip's time left current and takes the notice away within 30 s of the reset. It shows only while Claude is in use (the provider, or Setup open): beside "OpenAI ready" a "5-hour limit 85% used" would read as OpenAI's.
- **The usage-limit card is both cards.** The task did not say which card. A review that stops at a usage limit puts up a card with Claude Code's own words (which name the limit and its reset) and the advice (`usageLimitCard`), and Setup's status card in the usage-limit view, which a connection test that stops at a limit also leads to, gives the same advice under its text. The advice depends on which limit was reached, from the last notice: Sonnet and Haiku can go on past a weekly Opus limit, Haiku and Opus past a weekly Sonnet limit (but not on Pro or Enterprise, where it is the weekly limit: Names), and any other model past the weekly model limit, which the model list's Fable makes real (`setup.9` review: it was told to wait); but a 5-hour or weekly limit holds every model, so there the advice is to wait, with Sonnet and Haiku to make the limits last, in the words of the Pro plan note (`proPlanNote`). A reached one-model limit's tooltip says which reviews stop, "so reviews with Opus stop until it resets" (Sonnet, "that model"), not "reviews with Claude", which contradicted the card. Only one of the plan's limits gets advice (`isPlanLimit`). Claude Code's words alone do not tell the plan's limits from the rest: "You've hit your" starts its plan-limit messages and also "You've hit your team's shared budget. Switch to another model … to continue.", "You've hit your monthly spend limit." and the channel's, and "You've reached your" only its message for usage credits on Fable, "You've reached your Fable limit." (2.1.302's `AS` and `VWo`). So words that already say to switch model, or a notice that says extra usage was paying or was itself the limit reached, rule the advice out; a notice that says a plan limit was reached rules it in; and otherwise only Claude Code's own names for the plan's limits do, "You've hit your" and "limit", "session limit", "weekly limit", "Opus limit", "Sonnet limit" or "Fable limit" (its `hie`; `setup.9` review: any message that started "You've hit your" or "You've reached your" counted, so a team's shared budget after an extra-usage notice got "Wait until the limit resets", against Claude Code's "Switch to another model"). The rest, a team's shared budget, a spend limit, usage credits with no plan limit reached, or a seat without usage, keep Claude Code's words alone, which say which limit it is and mostly what to do. Running out of credits is not the line: when a plan limit and the extra usage behind it run out together, 2.1.302 says "You're out of usage credits · resets 3pm" with the plan's reset after a notice that names the plan's limit, and waiting for that reset lifts it, so the advice is given (`setup.9` said such messages were ones "waiting does not mend"). Setup says what the card said: its view cannot read Claude Code's message later, so `useAi` records the card's decision with the failure, from the same message and notice read at once (`failureSetupEvent`, `LastFailure.planLimit`; a failed connection test, from its own message and notice), and the view gives the advice only for a plan limit, and for any other a text that does not say the plan's limit was reached, as does the review dialog's warning (`setup.9` review: Setup gave the wait advice for every usage limit, credits and extra usage included).
- **The card reads the notice from a ref.** Main broadcasts the notice before it answers the review with the failure, but the page's state may not be drawn by the time the failure is handled, so `useAi` keeps each notice in a ref as it arrives as well as in its state, and the card reads `latestRateLimit()`.
- **The window check** handed the notices to the page's own state over DevTools, as main's broadcast would, in a `dev:clean --quiet --no-claude-code` window, so no request was made: the words, the tooltips, both themes, the expiry, OpenAI hiding it, and the card. The review fix's check did the same with a ready runtime on a Pro plan and the last failure handed to the page's state too, which reaches Setup's usage-limit view that `--no-claude-code` otherwise hides, before the fix and after it (`setup.9` task note). A notice from a real Claude Code was last seen in `setup.8`'s test.

### C.65 Open in Obsidian names no vault (`setup.10b`, 2026-10-18)

- **The link.** `obsidian://open?path=<full path>`, the form `setup.10` already used. Obsidian's help (https://obsidian.md/help/Extending+Obsidian/Obsidian+URI, read 2026-10-18) gives `vault` (a vault's name or id) with `file` (a path from the vault's root), or `path`, "an absolute file system path", which overrides both and makes Obsidian "search for the most specific vault which contains the specified file path". The app knows the file's full path but not the vault: the manuscript folder can be a vault or any folder inside one, Obsidian's own list of vaults is in its settings, which the app does not read, and looking upwards for a `.obsidian` folder misses a vault whose settings folder was renamed in Obsidian. A vault named by its folder could also be the wrong one of two folders with the same name. So the link carries the path alone, and the tests show that a vault folder's name, whatever it holds, is only part of that one value.
- **Encoding.** Obsidian asks for every value to be percent-encoded, slashes as %2F and spaces as %20, and warns that a reserved character left as it is "may break the interpretation of the URI". `encodeURIComponent` leaves `!'()*` alone, so they are encoded too; everything but `A-Z a-z 0-9 -._~` is percent-encoded as UTF-8. On Windows the backslashes stay in the path, as %5C: Obsidian's forum (https://forum.obsidian.md/t/starting-obsidian-from-command-line-with-uri-open-path-is-not-working-under-windows/107025, read 2026-10-18) has `obsidian://open/?path=C%3A%5CUsers%5C...%5Cexample%20note.md` working for a note in a vault Obsidian knows, while the same thread's `path=` to a folder, or to a vault Obsidian did not have in its list, failed ("Unable to find a vault for the URL"). Accented letters are left in whichever Unicode form the system gave them (NFC on Windows as a rule, NFD from some macOS calls), since the path has to match the vault's path as Obsidian holds it, and the tests show both survive the round trip.
- **What main hands the shell.** Only a link that `isObsidianOpenUrl` accepts: one whose value decodes to a full path to a `.md` file that `obsidianOpenUrl` encodes back to exactly the same link. So another scheme, another action, a second parameter, a fragment, lower-case escapes or a relative path never reach `shell.openExternal` this way. A `.txt` guidelines file, which the app otherwise accepts, gets Open only: Obsidian does not show `.txt` files, the button is disabled with that reason as its tooltip, and `guidelines:open` refuses it before `ensureGuidelinesFile` could create it (`openGuidelines`, which `test/main/guidelines.test.ts` runs on a `GUIDELINES.txt` that does not exist: refused, no file, no folder, no shell call). When the system cannot start anything for the link, the error says to check that Obsidian is installed or to use Open, with the system's words as the detail.
- **Blank.** The task says the note shows when `styleSheet` is `blank`. `guidelines:status` reports a file that does not exist yet as `missing`, but the review creates it from the template, whose style sheet is blank, and that first review is when the note matters most, so `styleSheetNote` shows it then too. A file of the author's own with no "## Style sheet" heading (`missing`, existing) gets none.
- **Settings' label.** "Open guidelines file" is kept for the system's app, since the README and section 7 name it; Review with AI says "Open", as the task gives it. Settings' row of four buttons would leave its path no room, so the two ways to open the file have a row of their own under it.
- **Not checked here.** No agent run starts Obsidian or an editor on the author's machine, so whether Obsidian opens the file is the author's check at sitting C, on the `apply.21` temporary vault.

### C.66 A shell that opens nothing (`setup.10b`, 2026-10-18)

The window check of `setup.10b` had to press Open in Obsidian and Open without starting Obsidian or an editor on a machine the author was using. The page cannot stand in for main: `window.api` is exposed by `contextBridge`, non-writable, non-configurable and frozen, so a drive cannot wrap `guidelines:open` in the page (tried first, and stopped before any button was pressed). Main can. A development switch, `MANUSCRIPT_REVIEW_DEV_SHELL_STUB` (`npm run dev:clean -- --shell-stub`), read in `src/main/devSwitches.ts` and ignored by a packaged build like every other switch (C.39), makes `systemShell()` in `src/main/ipc.ts` return `recordingShell` (`src/main/shellApi.ts`) in place of Electron's `shell`: `openExternal`, `openPath` and `showItemInFolder` open nothing and print `[shell stub] openExternal <link>` and so on to the terminal, and `openPath` answers as if it had worked. Every place main hands something to the system goes through `systemShell()`: `guidelines:open`, `shell:reveal` (Show in folder, Show backups, a set-aside text) and the window's web links in `index.ts`; `test/main/shellApi.test.ts` checks that neither file calls Electron's `shell` directly. Main prints "[shell stub] on: nothing is opened outside the app" as it starts, so a drive reads that line from the terminal log before it presses anything, rather than trusting the flag it passed. The folder picker is a dialog, not the shell, and is not covered.

### C.67 The Pro preselection belongs to the first welcome guide (`setup.11` review, 2026-10-19)

`proPreselection` (`setup.1`) took `onboardingDone: false` to mean a new user. Until `setup.11` nothing wrote that flag, so on a fresh install it stayed false for good, and Sonnet was preselected at the first check that found Claude ready on a Pro plan, whenever that came. `setup.11` writes the flag both ways, which changed this in two places.

- **Show the welcome guide again.** It wrote `onboardingDone: false` alone, so an install on Pro whose model was still Default, from before 1.0 or a fresh one that had skipped the guide, looked new, and the next check moved it to Sonnet (seen in a `dev:clean` window with a Pro plan's check put into the page: `default` became `sonnet` on disk). Section 1 says a settings file that names a model keeps it and that only a new user gets Sonnet. Show again now adds `pro-model-preselected` to `dismissedHints` in the same write (`welcomeAgainPatch`), so the guide comes back with the preselection settled. It is one write, so no settings reply holds `false` without the id.
- **Start or Skip before Claude is ready.** The guide invites starting with Connect Claude still to do. Two ways were open: keep the preselection pending until the first check that finds Claude ready, or let it belong to the first guide. Pending needs a mark that survives a restart and tells a fresh install that skipped from settings from before 1.0, which normalisation makes alike (`onboardingDone` true, no hints): a new settings key, or a "pending" id in a list of dismissed hints, which reads backwards. Chosen: the preselection happens only while the first guide is open (`proPreselection`'s `guideOpen`, App's `welcomeOpen`, which also counts a Start or Skip whose write failed as closed). After the guide has gone, a model setting never changes behind the author's back, and a Pro user left on Default still has the review dialog's note, beside the model list, that Opus models, Default included, use the limits fastest.

### C.68 Small changes are drawn bigger, never stored bigger (`small`, 2026-10-19)

The author's round 6 screenshot showed two changes trimmed to one character (4.3, step 5): an accepted hyphen-to-space in "double standard", a 4 px grey line under a space with a faint "WAS -" whose strike-through merged into the hyphen, and a pending comma after "Mira", a 2 px purple caret with a 3 px comma above it. On the line each was about 5 by 22 CSS px to hit, against 68 by 21 for the word "standard". Widening the stored hunk would have moved offsets, demoted neighbours in `resolveOverlaps`, let `touches()` supersede it more often and made the ghost repeat the whole word, so every change in 11.7 is drawn and nothing is stored: Save to chapter, Copy result, find and a copied paragraph read exactly as before, which `test/renderer/hunkDom.render.test.ts` guards for every shape and status.

Measured in quiet `dev:clean` windows on a fixture chapter with every shape of 11.7's table in each status, a word change with a comma touching it, two small changes two words apart and a dialogue line with six:

- **Caret.** Across 651 window widths from 900 to 1550 CSS px at 100%, the inline-block caret left the line of the character before it at 4 widths (after "it" in the dialogue line); the inline one at 0.8em at none, 2 by 15 px, and none at 80% or 200% either.
- **Targets.** A hit test at every CSS pixel of the line around each change: 34 small changes, 16 to 17 by 23 to 25 CSS px at 100%, 16 to 17 by 22 to 23 at 80% and 15 to 16 by 21 to 24 at 200%; case 2, a 4 px caret, is now 16 by 23. Word changes, 45 to 65 px wide, are as they were.
- **Capsule.** 12.75 CSS px wide at 100%. With every paragraph isolated, `.center` keeps its `OverflowScrolling` layer and LCD text (`none`, contents opaque) through 25 wheel steps.
- **Tints.** `--tint-small` 16% and `--tint-small-hover` 26% in both themes, by `theme.8`'s measure (11.3 e).
- **Keys.** With a box on every key, an accepted comma's "ADDED [,]" ran 11.6 px over the next "-> [I]" in the dialogue line at 100% and 200%; with the box kept only for named whitespace the two end 0.2 px apart.
- **Margin marks.** CSS anchor positioning works in Electron 44.3.0 with the isolation in place: each mark is 11 by 39.1 CSS px, flush against the text, its middle within 2 px of its line's at 80%, 100% and 200%. A paragraph join's live text is the break, whose box Chromium puts on the blank line between the paragraphs, so its bar sits there.
- **List rows.** In the default window a decided row has about 150 CSS px for its text beside its status, so a small change's row shows two words a side, at most 14 characters, around an inline chip, which the row's ellipsis cuts into rather than dropping whole as it dropped an inline-block one.

Found by the review (2026-10-19), each checked in a quiet `dev:clean` window at 100% on the `small.11` fixture before and after the fix:

- **A bar's hover card stayed open.** The arrowhead was a `span` under the same key as the bar's `button`, so when a bar became the arrowhead React replaced the element, and fired no leave for the one that had gone. Resting on a bar until its card opened and clicking it, a quick click before the card's 100 ms, or focus by the store (as Shift+J or a list row would give it) each left the card open over the next paragraphs with the pointer elsewhere, through a wheel scroll too, until another suggestion was hovered. The arrowhead is now the same button, with no pointer: after the click the card closed with the pointer still, the quick click opened none, and focus by the store closed it as the pointer left. A mark that unmounts under the pointer closes its card itself (`markHover`, whose `gone` the mark calls as it unmounts): a bar whose change was decided under the resting pointer closed its card at once, and with `gone` made to do nothing the card stayed open after the pointer left. After Save to chapter the paragraph's bar was a new element and the card closed as the pointer left. The arrowhead still measures 11 by 39.09 CSS px, its middle 0.55 px from its line.
- **A focused small change's ghost sat on `--focus-bg`.** Category text on the dark focus yellow is 4.14:1 for spelling, 4.18 homophone, 4.28 punctuation and 4.03 other, under the 4.5 a category as text needs (light 4.70 and up), and no pair checked it. Compared in both themes on a spelling and a punctuation change: the fill dulled the ghost in dark, and a ring with no fill read as a keycap round the whole ghost, which since `small.7` means named whitespace. The ghost is now unfilled, as a word-sized change's always was; the capsule's yellow, the arrowhead and the x mark the focus.

Questions for the author, with the contact sheet of `small.11`: whether case 1 bothered him because the change was hard to find again or because "WAS -" was unreadable; "period" or "full stop" (the app says "full stop"); whether a click on a bar should accept the change instead of selecting it; and whether the page stays calm with a page full of commas.

### C.69 Default is Opus 5.5 on Pro since CLI 2.1.280 (`setup.13`, 2026-10-19)

Section 1 said that what Default means depends on the account, "Opus on Max, Sonnet on Pro". That was true of the Claude Code the plan was written against, and stopped being true at 2.1.280; 11.1's Default model row had already given the newer fact, with the reason the Pro note matters.

- **What Anthropic's page says** (model-config, re-read 2026-10-19, Appendix A). With no model chosen, Claude Code's Default is Opus 5.5 on Pro, Max, Team, Enterprise and the Anthropic API. Before 2.1.280 it was Sonnet 5 on Pro and on Team Standard seats, and Opus 5 on the rest. The same version moved the `opus` alias to Opus 5.5; `sonnet` became Sonnet 5.5 at 2.1.284 and `haiku` Haiku 5.5 at 2.1.293. No plan's Default is a Fable model.
- **What it means for the app.** A new settings file says `model: 'default'`, which the app passes as no `--model` flag at all (`modelArgs`, `setup.1`). On Claude Code 2.1.280 or newer a review on a Pro plan therefore runs on Opus 5.5, which uses a Pro plan's limits fastest; before, it ran on Sonnet 5. Two things stand between a Pro user and that: the first welcome guide preselects Sonnet when its check finds a Pro plan ready and the model is still Default (`proPreselection`, C.67), and the review dialog's note beside the model list, "On a Pro plan, Opus models (including Default) use your limits fastest. Sonnet is usually enough for copyediting." (`PRO_PLAN_NOTE`), shows for Default and every Opus model. Section 1's row now says this.
- **What else decides Default, as the app runs Claude Code.** The page orders the choices: `--model` (the app passes it for every model but Default), `ANTHROPIC_MODEL` (removed from the child's environment, 11.3 c), a `model` in a settings file, which `/model` writes (not read, as the app passes `--setting-sources=`), then `ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_MODEL` (2.1.236 and later), which passes through unchanged, so a user who set it gets that model as Default in the app too. An Enterprise organisation's default model (2.1.196 and later) and a model recorded on an Enterprise account by `/model` (2.1.280 and later) also change it. The model list labels Default with what Claude Code's `initialize` reply says it is on that machine and account (round 5), so the review dialog shows the model Default stands for.
- **Between 2.1.266 and 2.1.279.** `MIN_CLAUDE_CODE` is 2.1.266, so a Claude Code the app accepts can still make Sonnet 5 a Pro plan's Default, and there the note's "(including Default)" was wrong. Such a copy is older than `RECOMMENDED_CLAUDE_CODE` (2.1.283), so the review dialog and Setup show the update note with `claude update`, and a copy from Anthropic's installer updates itself; `setup.13` left the note as it was. `setup.17` makes it follow the model list, whose Default entry is labelled from the `initialize` reply (round 5): `defaultIsOpus` reads the family from that label ("Default: Sonnet 5"), and where it is not Opus, `proPlanNote` shows nothing while Default is chosen and, for an Opus model, "On a Pro plan, Opus models use your limits fastest. Sonnet is usually enough for copyediting." (`PRO_PLAN_NOTE_OPUS_ONLY`), without the parenthesis. Where the list has not come yet, or its Default label names no version, the note stays as it was, since Default is Opus 5.5 on every plan from 2.1.280. The same rule covers an `ANTHROPIC_DEFAULT_MODEL` that makes Default something other than Opus. Left as it is: the usage-limit card's advice for a limit every model shares (`WAIT_ADVICE`, `setup.9`) also says "Opus models (including Default)", shown on any plan after a review stopped at a limit; it is built where no model list is read (`usageLimitCard`, `setupStatus`), and on such a copy the update note already shows; a `MIN_CLAUDE_CODE` of 2.1.280 or later would retire the case for both.
- **Checked against the installed Claude Code** (2026-10-19; `claude --version` and `claude --help`, with no session started): 2.1.302, newer than 2.1.280. Its `--help` describes `--model` as an alias for the latest model, giving `fable`, `opus` and `sonnet` as examples, or a model's full name, and says nothing about the account's default, which only the `initialize` reply and the page give. This machine is signed in on a Max plan, so a Pro plan's Default was not seen; no review ran and no model list was asked for.

### C.70 No in-app sign-in launcher (`setup.13`, 2026-10-19)

11.0 cuts it in one line ("the legal page bars offering Claude login. Setup shows a command to copy."). This records what was weighed and what the app does instead.

- **What a launcher would have been.** A button on Setup or in the welcome guide that signs the author in: running `claude auth login` as a child of the app, opening a terminal with the command already typed, or opening the browser at Anthropic's sign-in page. Each makes the sign-in start from the app.
- **Why not.** The legal page (re-read 2026-10-19, Appendix A) says Anthropic does not let third-party developers offer Claude.ai login in their own applications, and that sign-in to a Claude account has to complete through Anthropic's own flow; it also says none of that stops an end user signing in to the unmodified Claude Code with their own subscription. A button in the app that starts the sign-in reads as the first; the user running Anthropic's command in their own terminal is the second. The Usage Policy that takes effect on 2026-11-12 also counts circumventing the log-in process among its bypasses (Appendix A). Nothing about the sign-in passes through the app: Claude Code keeps the login in `%USERPROFILE%\.claude\.credentials.json` (authentication page), which the app never reads (CLAUDE.md rule 2 (a); `test/shared/policy.test.ts` keeps the credential file's name, `setup-token` and `logout` out of the source).
- **What the app does instead** (5.6). Setup shows the view's command, `claude auth login` or the one line that installs Claude Code and signs in, with Copy, on the tab of the user's system, and the full-path form where a terminal may not find `claude`. The author runs it in their own terminal, where Claude Code opens the browser at Anthropic's page, or prints the link and reads a pasted code. Setup checks again as its window comes back to the front, and Test the connection shows a request going through. `runClaudeCli` refuses every command but `--version` and `auth status --json`, and the tests of Setup and the welcome guide (`setupScreen.render.test.ts`, `welcome.test.ts`) find no button labelled like a sign-in.
- **Checked against the installed Claude Code** (2.1.302, 2026-10-19; help output only, no sign-in started). `claude --help` lists `auth` and `setup-token`; `claude auth --help` lists `login`, `logout` and `status`; `claude auth login --help` gives `--claudeai` (a Claude subscription, the default), `--console` (Console billing), `--email` and `--sso`; `claude auth status --help` gives `--json` (the default) and `--text`. So the commands Setup shows exist as written, and `claude auth login` with no flag signs in with a Claude plan. The top-level help also says `--bare` takes Anthropic sign-in only from `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` or an `apiKeyHelper` and never reads OAuth, which is why the app never passes it (CLAUDE.md rule 6).
- **The cost.** A trip to a terminal on the first run, and again whenever a sign-in ends. The install line signs in as soon as it has installed, so a first run is one paste, and Setup's Troubleshooting covers a browser that does not open.

### C.71 An ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN is the token, not a Claude plan (`setup.17`, 2026-10-19)

The `setup.13` review left open how the check reads an `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`, the bearer token that Anthropic's authentication page ranks above an API key and the subscription sign-in, for a gateway or proxy (Appendix A, Authentication). `setup.17` added `--auth-token-dummy` to `dev:clean`, which sets it to a dummy token as `--api-key-dummy` sets a dummy key, and looked.

- **What Claude Code says** (2.1.302, 2026-10-19). Its two answers were also read outside the app, with the app's flags and no prompt, in an empty configuration folder of the scratch directory's own (`auth status --json` and the `initialize` exchange; dummy token and key; only the fields the app keeps were printed). With the token, `auth status` gives `loggedIn` true, `authMethod` `oauth_token`, `apiProvider` `firstParty` and no key; the `initialize` account gives `tokenSource` `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` and `apiProvider`, with no email, plan or key. Over this machine's own Max sign-in the token wins: the same answers, no email, and a line on stderr that the claude.ai connectors are off because another auth source takes precedence over the claude.ai login. Beside an `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, both answers add `apiKeySource` `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, and `auth status` still says `oauth_token`. For comparison: signed out, `tokenSource` `none`; the key alone, `tokenSource` `none` with the key's source; and this machine's plain sign-in, `email`, `organization`, `subscriptionType` (`Claude Max`) and `apiProvider`, with neither `tokenSource` nor `apiKeySource`. So 2.1.302 never gives the `apiKeySource` `none` that made Settings' Claude account row read "key from none" (`test/main/claudeRuntime.test.ts` keeps one in its fixture; the row now passes over it, `setup.17` (d)).
- **What the app made of it.** In a quiet `dev:clean --auth-token-dummy --claude-config <empty folder>` window, before the fix: ready, with the credential `subscription`, from `oauth_token`; no launch card; the status bar "Claude ready"; Setup "Claude is ready", "Claude Code 2.1.302 is signed in. Review with AI can use Claude.", Advanced "a Claude plan, auth method oauth_token" and Test the connection's note "counts against your plan"; Settings' row "signed in". The token is not a plan's: it displaces the sign-in, and its requests are billed to whoever issued it. So `classifyClaude` misread it, and `setup.17` fixes that.
- **The fix.** `ClaudeCredential` gains `auth-token`, which `classifyClaude` gives when the account's `tokenSource` is `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` and no cloud provider is named, ahead of the API key rule, in the page's order (cloud provider, token, key). The view stays `ready`, so the welcome guide and the launch card are unchanged; the card says "Claude Code 2.1.302 signs in with the token in ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN, such as a gateway or proxy hands out, so reviews are billed to the account behind that token, not through your Claude sign-in. Review with AI can use Claude." and "To use your Claude sign-in instead, remove ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN from your environment and start <APP_NAME> again."; the status bar "Claude ready (token)"; Advanced "a token from ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN, auth method oauth_token"; the test note "Sends one very small request to Claude, which is billed to the account behind your ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN like any other."; Settings' row "signed in, token from ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN". The same window after the fix showed exactly these. Without the `initialize` account, when only `auth status` answers, `oauth_token` still reads as a plan, as nothing there tells the token from a plan's own.
- **Not done.** No model request was sent: the check sends none and Test the connection was not pressed, so how a request with a token that is refused fails is not known. `buildClaudeEnv` passes `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` through unchanged, as it does `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` (11.3 c), so a review uses it as the check found it.

## Appendix D: release 1.0 answers

The author's answers to 11.6, dated, numbered as there. A question not listed here is still open.

- **1. Name** (2026-10-14): **Lapidary.** The author had become unsure about Igneous once it turned out to be in use elsewhere, and chose Lapidary from this research:
  - **Trademarks.** Rubrik's software mark IGNEOUS (US Reg. 5926931) was cancelled on 2026-06-19. Every live IGNEOUS mark in the US and Canada covers unrelated goods.
  - **Same niche.** Two free Markdown apps named Igneous, both aimed at Obsidian users, launched in the ten days before: a notes app for iPhone, iPad and Mac that opens Obsidian folders (getigneous.com, 2026-10-07), and a GNOME desktop editor described as an alternative frontend to Obsidian (github.com/H4RL33/igneous, 2026-10-05).
  - **Recommendation.** Rename for confusion and discoverability, not legal risk. **Lapidary** is first: the craft of cutting and polishing stone, obsidian included, and "lapidary" prose is polished and concise. It has no software trademark, no Obsidian plugin or theme, no notable app, and lapidary.app is taken. **Ventifact**, a stone polished by wind-blown sand over years, is the cleanest name but obscure. **Stet** looked clean on narrow checks only.
  - **Ruled out.** Whetstone, which is crowded with 2026 AI writing tools. Basalt, Pumice, Granite and Onyx, which are already Obsidian plugin or theme names.
- **2. Logo** (2026-10-14): concept A "Seam" is the direction, but with a more rock-like body, like obsidian, in place of the flat basalt fill. The squarish-round pebble shape stays. Three variants with the same rim and foreground are in `dist/logo-concepts/rock/`, with `compare.png` and each folder's `render.py`:
  - **conchoidal:** black glass with curved fracture ripples, ranked first as the most rock-like;
  - **polished-glass:** the cleanest at small sizes, but reads more like a black gem;
  - **golden-sheen:** reads as amber.

  At 16 to 32 px all three become a glossy black pebble with the ember rim; texture shows from 48 px. The author did not take these. He asked instead for a round rock divided into sections by fracture lines, after his own sketch, with Seam's foreground on top, in orange or perhaps a turquoise or cyan theme. If none of those suits, the original Seam stays. Prototypes go in `dist/logo-concepts/sections/`.
  - **Decided later on 2026-10-14: the original Seam, in turquoise** (rim #5ad3db to #178e9c, body #29393c to #121b1d, ghost #7d999b, line #f1efe6, dots #4fd8e0), drawn by `dist/logo-concepts/seam-colours/render.py` as palette 2. The ember original is the alternative he may return to. The sectioned-rock prototypes in `dist/logo-concepts/sections/` were not taken.
- **3. Repository** (2026-10-14): yes to a fresh public repository, `lapidary`, but later. `opensource.9` waits until the author asks for it.
- **8. Anthropic's Commercial Terms** (2026-10-14): **not accepted.** He will not make money from the app and will not bind himself legally for a free tool a handful of people may use. Each user installs Claude Code themselves and uses their own subscription or key; the app only runs the Claude Code the user installed. Until then the SDK had bundled `claude.exe` into the installer; the plan now ships no Claude Code binary (11.1, `setup.3`, `release.4`). Whether the SDK's JavaScript library also goes is question 21. The research behind the question is in Appendix A.
- **13. Setup** (2026-10-14): settled by question 8. Setup leads with installing Claude Code, then signing in.
- **Questions 4 to 7, 9 to 12 and 14 to 22** (2026-10-14): all as recommended.
  - **4. Identity.** Public commits and the package author use the GitHub noreply address; the author turns on "Keep my email address private" and "Block command line pushes that expose my email" when the public repo is made. Commits in the private repo keep his usual identity, because the public repo starts from a fresh root commit.
  - **5. Licence.** MIT, "Copyright (c) 2026 Michael Bradet-Legris", with his name as the installer's publisher.
  - **6. AGENTS.md.** Replaced by a pointer to CLAUDE.md (`opensource.7`).
  - **7. Commits.** The agent commits locally on a branch at the end of each milestone; the author reviews, merges and pushes. Agents never push. **Changed later on 2026-10-14:** the agent may commit and push. It works on a branch per milestone, pushes it, and fast-forwards `master` once the milestone verifies; it never force-pushes.
  - **9. Theme.** Yes to the darker light-theme tokens (#8a8378, #84681c), links in the app's blue, "Match system" as the default, and the warm basalt dark palette.
  - **10. Backups.** The newest 20 saves plus the first of each day for 30 days, in the app's data folder.
  - **11. Save.** Ctrl+S saves to the chapter, the button reads "Save N changes to chapter", leaving a chapter always asks, and there is no automatic save to chapter.
  - **12. Model.** The author's own setting moves from `opus` to Default; a new user on Pro gets Sonnet preselected, with the limits note.
  - **14. Updates.** Check at start-up and daily, with a toggle in About; download only on a click.
  - **15. Signing.** Decided only if Smart App Control actually blocks the friend; `release.14` stays conditional. No paid Apple program.
  - **16. OpenAI.** Ships labelled experimental; `hardening.11b` is cut.
  - **17. Diagnostics.** May include folder paths with the username replaced by `~`, chapter file names and the chapter count.
  - **18. Usage.** The implementer may spend a little of the author's Claude usage on verification: a few tiny reviews and connection tests.
  - **19. Sittings.** Yes to all four.
  - **20. Reports.** The friend messages the author on Discord or opens a GitHub issue; the issue form stays.
  - **21. SDK library.** Removed. The app runs the user's installed `claude` command directly (11.3 q, `cli.1`), so nothing of Anthropic's ships.
  - **22. Colour.** Turquoise for now, chosen with the logo (question 2); the ember palette may come back later.
- **Friend defaults** (2026-10-14, replacing the `files.17` questionnaire). The author would rather not question his friend, so the plan assumes:
  - **Platform.** Windows 11 x64. Smart App Control is handled only if he reports a block. macOS and Linux builds are cut (`release.16`, `release.17`, `brand.10`) unless he turns out to need one.
  - **Obsidian syntax** is shown as plain text, as now, with nothing rendered. The only special handling is that the reviewer is told to leave it alone, and a suggestion that would change the syntax itself, such as the target inside `[[...]]`, is kept out, so links and embeds keep working (`files.6` to `files.8`). Dataview and Templater get no special handling.
  - **Files.** One chapter per file, so `files.14` is cut. Save does not keep the file's creation date (`files.15` cut; the README says so). A chapter renamed while open is found again when reopened (`files.1`), so `files.18` is cut.
  - **Sync.** Any tool; the README's sync advice covers them.
  - **First use.** No sample chapter or screen share: the author sends the release link (`files.13`).
  - **No AI subscription.** He can use the OpenAI provider with a pay-as-you-go API key from platform.openai.com (a ChatGPT subscription cannot be used by other apps), or Claude Code with an Anthropic API key. At the 2026-09-11 prices this is roughly 5 US cents per chapter with gpt-5-mini and up to about a dollar with the largest models, depending on length and effort. The README and the Setup screen say so.

## Appendix A: billing and policy sources (checked 2026-09-08, re-checked 2026-10-16, Claude Code setup pages re-read 2026-10-19)

The list below is the 2026-09-08 check. Every page in it still loaded on 2026-10-16; what has changed since, and what the release depends on, is in the subsection after it. Five Claude Code pages that the Setup screen and the README rest on were re-read on 2026-10-19 for `setup.13`, in the subsection after that.

- Help Center, "Use the Claude Agent SDK with your Claude plan": update dated June 15 says the planned change is paused; Agent SDK, `claude -p`, and third-party app usage still draw from the subscription's usage limits. https://support.claude.com/en/articles/15036540-use-the-claude-agent-sdk-with-your-claude-plan
- Claude Code docs, Legal and compliance: OAuth is for subscription holders' ordinary use of Claude Code and native apps; developers may not offer claude.ai login in their own products or intermediate credentials; an end user signing in to the unmodified Claude Code binary with their own subscription is allowed. https://code.claude.com/docs/en/legal-and-compliance
- Agent SDK overview and quickstart: bundled native binary, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` as the documented developer path. https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/overview and https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/quickstart
- Authentication: credential precedence, Windows storage location. https://code.claude.com/docs/en/authentication
- Structured outputs, streaming, cost tracking, TypeScript reference: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/structured-outputs , https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/streaming-output , https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/cost-tracking , https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/typescript

### Anthropic terms for a free open-source release (research dated 2026-10-14, re-read 2026-10-16; 11.6 question 8)

Quotes were checked by a second, independent pass on 2026-10-14, and again on 2026-10-16 against copies of the pages saved that day. This is information for the author, not legal advice.

- **Re-read on 2026-10-16.** Every page below loaded, and none has changed in a way that matters here since the 2026-10-14 research. Each was compared with its newest Wayback capture, dated 2026-10-04 to 2026-10-14; the Consumer Terms and Commercial Terms only by their unchanged effective dates. The legal page's only change since 2026-08-30 is its Healthcare compliance (BAA) section, rewritten between 2026-10-04 and 2026-10-08. The bullets below also record text that was already on these pages on 2026-10-14 but was left out then.
- **Legal and compliance page.** Running Claude Code "in your products or services" requires agreeing to the Commercial Terms "unless we've mutually agreed otherwise". The conditions are: the binary is unmodified, no built-in sign-in method is restricted, and no paying for, reselling or intermediating users' usage. The page's only example of such a product is hosted sandboxes or other agent infrastructure. The same page says Anthropic does not permit "third-party developers to offer Claude.ai login into their own applications", but does not prevent an end user "signing in to the unmodified Claude Code binary with their own Claude subscription". That section first appears in Wayback between 2026-08-16 and 2026-08-30. A 2026-02-18 capture instead said subscription OAuth tokens in any other tool, "including the Agent SDK", were not permitted; that sentence was gone by 2026-04-13. https://code.claude.com/docs/en/legal-and-compliance
  - **Using the Claude Code name and logo.** A product may say accurately, in plain text, that it has Claude Code preinstalled or that it "runs Claude Code". It may not use the Claude Code or Anthropic names or logos in its own product, feature or company name, in its logo, or in a way that suggests Anthropic built, endorses or partners with it. Any other use of Anthropic's names or logos falls under the Trademark Guidelines and needs written permission. The paragraph is in every capture from 2026-08-30 on. It sits in the section on products that run Claude Code under the Commercial Terms, and it is the only Anthropic text found on plain-text mentions; the plan follows it as guidance. Its rule is the plan's: no Claude or Anthropic marks in the product name or logo, and Claude Code named only in plain text. `DISCLAIMER` uses the page's own wording, that the app runs Claude Code.
  - **Also on the page since at least 2026-08-30, not recorded before.** Developers may not route requests through Free, Pro or Max plan credentials on behalf of their users. They may not collect, store or intermediate Claude.ai credentials or session tokens, and sign-in to a Claude account "must complete through Anthropic's own flow". Anthropic may enforce these restrictions "without prior notice". The advertised Pro and Max limits "assume ordinary, individual usage" of Claude Code and the Agent SDK. Under License, Free, Pro and Max users are under the Consumer Terms, and Team, Enterprise and API users under the Commercial Terms.
- **Anthropic Trademark Guidelines** (effective 2024-08-01; identical to the 2026-10-04 capture). They cover Anthropic's names, logos, brand elements and anything it uses to identify its products. Anthropic's marks may be used only as it specifically permits, in materials it approves beforehand, and never in a way that implies sponsorship, endorsement, or a relationship or affiliation with Anthropic. Marks may not be altered or carry a trademark symbol. The guidelines say nothing about plain-text mentions of a product; the legal page's paragraph above is the only text found on that case. https://www.anthropic.com/legal/trademark-guidelines
- **Consumer Terms** (effective 2025-10-08), which govern Free, Pro and Max users of Claude Code. Section 3 bars accessing the Services "through automated or non-human means", except with an Anthropic API key or "where we otherwise explicitly permit it". The app runs `claude -p` as a child process, which is scripted access. The texts treated as that explicit permission are the legal page's clause that an end user may sign in to the unmodified binary with their own subscription, and the Help Center's 2026-10-07 statement that `claude -p` and third-party apps can still use subscription limits. Section 12, "Use of our brand", bars using Anthropic's name, logos or trademarks in connection with other products, or in a way that implies affiliation, endorsement or sponsorship, without prior written permission. The legal page's name-and-logo paragraph is the only Anthropic text that addresses a plain-text statement that a product runs Claude Code; whether its section applies to this app is the open question in section 9 risk 8, bullet 4. https://www.anthropic.com/legal/consumer-terms
- **Agent SDK overview and quickstart** (actively maintained; reworded between April and August 2026 and kept after the August rewrite of the legal page; on 2026-10-16 the overview matched its 2026-10-10 capture, and the quickstart differed from its 2026-10-04 capture by one word). Unless previously approved, Anthropic does not allow third-party developers "to offer claude.ai login or rate limits for their products", agents built on the SDK included, and points them to API keys instead. The SDK "is governed by Anthropic's Commercial Terms of Service", including in products offered to others. https://code.claude.com/docs/en/agent-sdk/overview
  - **Branding guidelines** on the overview page, written for partners integrating the Agent SDK. Claude branding is optional. Allowed: "Claude Agent", "Claude" inside a menu already labelled "Agents", and "{YourAgentName} Powered by Claude". Not permitted: "Claude Code" or "Claude Code Agent", or visuals that mimic Claude Code. The product keeps its own branding and must not appear to be Claude Code or any Anthropic product. These rules address SDK integrations, which the app no longer is (C.42, Appendix D 21). It meets the last one anyway: its name, logo and window are its own, and its provider label is the plain "Claude". A GitHub issue asking for the same guidance for projects that run the Claude Code CLI, anthropics/claude-code#24600, was closed as not planned on 2026-03-11 with no answer from Anthropic.
- **Commercial Terms** (effective 2025-06-17; no newer version posted on 2026-10-16). Nothing in them depends on charging money, and no page exempts free or open-source projects. The author would be the Customer and the people he gives the app to his Users. Acceptance is free: signing in to the Console says "By continuing, you agree". D.3: the Customer must notify Users that factual assertions in outputs should be checked before they are relied on; the README's `AI_OUTPUT_NOTICE` says so anyway, as plain good practice. K.2: the Customer defends Anthropic against third-party claims about the Customer's or Users' inputs, or about use that breaches the Usage Policy, and pays resulting judgments or approved settlements. This duty is uncapped (L.3 excludes it), while other liability is capped both ways at fees paid. Section F grants no rights by implication; the legal page's clause is the only text that permits shipping `claude.exe`. https://www.anthropic.com/legal/commercial-terms
- **Usage Policy** effective 2026-11-12, published on anthropic.com on 2026-10-08 and so not yet in force on 2026-10-16. The version it replaces, effective 2025-09-15, has no such clause. Under "Do Not Abuse Our Platform" it bars reselling, proxying or otherwise providing access to Claude through unauthorised means, including services that "route requests through consumer subscriptions or misrepresent the product or client being used". The app runs the real `claude` binary and never sets or overrides its client or entrypoint identity (CLAUDE.md rule 6).
